Unbounded Weight Structures: (Re)construction and Completion
Thomas Nikolaus, Phil Pützstück

Abstract
. We develop a theory of completeness for weight structures on stable categories, dual to the theory of complete t t -structures. As in the bounded case, we show that complete weight structures are determined by their weight heart, giving rise to a universal construction 𝒜 ↦ K ⁡ ( 𝒜 ) \mathcal{A}\mapsto K(\mathcal{A}) that assigns a complete weight category to an additive category and recovers classical examples such as homotopy categories of chain complexes. We also give a general construction of weight structures on presentable stable categories generated by a small set of objects, generalizing a result of Bondarko. This recovers the standard weight structure on spectra and an exotic one related to Anderson duality. We identify their completions with Bousfield–Kan completions arising in Adams-type spectral sequences. To treat naturally occurring examples - such as derived categories of abelian categories and module categories over ring spectra - which are often only partially weight complete, we introduce the notion of weak t-structures. Within this framework, we prove that any stable category equipped with compatible weight and weak t-structures, and satisfying left weight completeness and right t-completeness, can be reconstructed from its heart via a two-step completion process 𝒜 ↦ K ^ ​ ( 𝒜 ) \mathcal{A}\mapsto\widehat{K}(\mathcal{A}) .



1.
Introduction

Many constructions in homotopy theory and algebra proceed by building
objects from simpler pieces. Classical examples include CW decompositions
of spaces or spectra and projective resolutions of chain complexes.
Such cell structures provide filtrations whose successive pieces may be
viewed as “pure” building blocks and are an indispensable tool in many constructions and computations.

Weight structures provide an abstract framework for organizing such
decompositions in stable categories. Introduced by Bondarko

[
9
]
, they can be viewed as a dual analogue of

t
-structures on triangulated categories or stable
\infty
-categories. Although
the formal definition appeared only relatively recently, many of the
underlying ideas were already present earlier in the literature, notably in
[
39
, Section 1.7]
,
[
6
, Section 4]
. This presents some important applications of weight structures
to algebraic
K
-theory. They have also been used recently, see e.g.
[
34
]
,
[
21
]
,
[
17
]
.

Concretely, a weight structure on a stable category
\mathcal{C}
is determined
by a full subcategory
\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}\subseteq\mathcal{C}
closed under retracts, extensions and finite colimits
such that every object
X\in\mathcal{C}
admits a
fiber sequence

X^{\prime}\to X\to X^{\prime\prime},

called a
weight decomposition
, where
X^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}
and

X^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}_{w\leq-1}\coloneqq{}^{\perp}(\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0})
1
1

1

Here

{}^{\perp}(\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0})
denotes the left orthogonal subcategory
consisting of objects that admit no nontrivial morphisms into objects of

\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}
.
. Intuitively, the objects
X^{\prime}
and
X^{\prime\prime}
describe the parts
of
X
of weight
\leq-1
and
\geq 0
, respectively.
Generally we set
\mathcal{C}_{\geq n}=\Sigma^{n}\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}
and
\mathcal{C}_{w\leq n}=\Sigma^{n}\mathcal{C}_{w\leq 0}
for any
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.
The
weight heart

\mathcal{C}^{w\heartsuit}\coloneqq\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}\cap\mathcal{C}_{w\leq 0},

consists of objects of pure weight
0
.

In many situations where weight structures arise—such as in algebraic

K
-theory—the ambient category
\mathcal{C}
is small and the weight structure
is
bounded
.
This means that every object lies in

\mathcal{C}_{[a,b]}\coloneqq\mathcal{C}_{\geq a}\cap\mathcal{C}_{w\leq b}
for some integers

a\leq b
, or equivalently, that every object has a finite filtration with associated graded in (shifts) of the heart
\mathcal{C}^{w\heartsuit}
.
In this case the category
\mathcal{C}
can be recovered from the heart as the
minimal stable category generated by
\mathcal{C}^{w\heartsuit}

[
34
, Proposition 3.3 and Corollary 3.4]
, see also Theorem
5.1
.
In this sense, bounded weight structures describe stable categories
that are minimally generated by their weight heart.

While the bounded case is by now well understood, considerably less is known about unbounded weight structures. This leads to the following natural question:

To what extent can a stable category be reconstructed from its weight heart in the absence of boundedness?

The present paper is devoted to this problem: we study unbounded weight structures and, in particular, focus on the opposite extreme to the bounded case: those that describe stable categories which are, in a precise sense, maximal among those admitting a given weight heart.
Our guiding principle is that a weight structure encodes how objects are assembled from the heart, while completeness determines how far this assembly process can be extended beyond the bounded setting.

Concretely, we introduce completeness conditions for weight structures,
analogous to those familiar from the theory of
t
-structures
[
5
]
,
[
27
]
,
[
22
, Section 1.2.1]
. Completeness
expresses that constructions from the heart can be carried out
indefinitely in a controlled way, and we show that complete weight
structures are again determined by their weight heart

\mathcal{C}^{w\heartsuit}
.
Completeness for weight
structures, similarly to the case of
t
-structures, comes in two
directions: left and right. Since right completeness for

\mathcal{C}
is equivalent to left completeness for the opposite category

\mathcal{C}^{\textsf{op}}
, we concentrate on the left version here. More precisely, we have:

Definition A

(Definition
4.1
)
.

A weight structure
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0})
is called left complete if

(1)

\mathcal{C}_{\geq\infty}\coloneqq\bigcap_{n\geq 0}\mathcal{C}_{\geq n}=0
.

(2)

\mathcal{C}
admits sequential colimits growing in connectivity, i.e. colimits of sequential diagrams where the successive cofibers become arbitrarily connective.

(3)

\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}\subseteq\mathcal{C}
is closed under such sequential colimits growing in connectivity.

We will see that by the Dold-Kan correspondence, (2) and (3) combined
are equivalent to the statement that
\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}
admits geometric realizations and that the inclusion
\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}\subseteq\mathcal{C}
preserves them (Lemma
2.20
).
Let us also mention that if
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0})
instead determines a
t
-structure,
and we replace colimits with limits in (2) and (3), then the above definition
precisely recovers left
t
-completeness (Lemma
2.25
).

We shall study many examples and see for instance that the usual weight structures
on spectra and the derived category of a ring are left complete, whereas those on finite spectra and the perfect derived category are not.
The main structural result of the paper is the following.

Theorem B

(Proposition
4.7
, Theorem
4.9
, Corollary
4.11
)
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a stable category with a weight structure.

(1)

There exists a “left completion”
\smash{\mathcal{C}}^{\uparrow}
of
\mathcal{C}
, i.e. a universal weight exact functor

\mathcal{C}\to\smash{\mathcal{C}}^{\uparrow}

to a left complete weight category which is an equivalence
if and only if
\mathcal{C}
is left complete.

(2)

The induced functor

\mathcal{C}^{w\heartsuit}\to(\smash{\mathcal{C}}^{\uparrow})^{w\heartsuit}
is an idempotent completion and
\mathcal{C}_{w<\infty}\to(\smash{\mathcal{C}}^{\uparrow})_{w<\infty}
is a fully faithful dense inclusion.

(3)

If
\mathcal{C}
is left complete, then the subcategory
\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}
is idempotent complete
and obtained from the weight heart by freely adjoining geometric realizations,
i.e.
\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}=\mathcal{P}^{\Delta^{\textsf{op}}}(\mathcal{C}^{w\heartsuit})
.

In particular, if
\mathcal{C}
is both left and right complete, then the subcategories
\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}
and
\mathcal{C}_{w\leq 0}
admit universal characterizations determined by the weight heart
\mathcal{C}^{w\heartsuit}
. In this case, the entire category
\mathcal{C}
can be recovered as the left and right completion of the bounded weight structure with the same heart, i.e. the stable envelope by Sosnilo’s result (Theorem
5.1
).

More generally, we denote by
K(\mathcal{A})
the
\infty
-category obtained from an additive
\infty
-category
\mathcal{A}
as the left and right completion of its stable envelope. The notation is inspired by a well-known classical construction: for an ordinary additive category
\mathcal{A}
, we show (Theorem
5.5
) that the
\infty
-category
K(\mathcal{A})
is equivalent to the
\infty
-category which has as objects chain complexes in
\mathcal{A}
and as mapping anima the underlying anima of the hom-complexes. In particular, its homotopy category is the classical homotopy category of chain complexes. This homotopy category is usually denoted by
K(\mathcal{A})

[
38
]
, but by abuse of notation, we use
K(\mathcal{A})
for the
\infty
-categorical analogue.
As a consequence of Theorem
B
we obtain the following.

Corollary C

(Theorem
5.4
)
.

The category
K(\mathcal{A})
only depends on the idempotent completion of
\mathcal{A}
. For idempotent complete additive categories the functor
\mathcal{A}\mapsto K(\mathcal{A})
is right adjoint to the functor
\mathcal{C}\mapsto(\mathcal{C}^{w\heartsuit})^{\textsf{idem}}
that assigns to a weight category the idempotent completion of its weight heart. Moreover this adjunction restricts to an equivalence between (left and right) complete weight categories and idempotent complete additive categories.

Examples and Applications

Our main motivation for this theory comes from a number of important examples, some of which we now discuss in order of increasing complexity.

The first example is the derived category of the integers
\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{Z})
with the weight structure induced from usual (
t
-)connective objects. The weight coconnectives
\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{Z})_{w\leq 0}
are given by those objects
C
with
H_{i}(C)=0
for
i>0
and
H_{0}(C)
a free abelian group.
This is left and right weight complete as one easily checks. The weight heart consists of the category of free abelian groups
\mathrm{Proj}_{\mathbb{Z}}
(not necessarily finitely generated) so that we recover the well-known equivalence

\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{Z})=K(\mathrm{Proj}_{\mathbb{Z}})\ .

The subcategory
\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{Z})^{\omega}
of perfect complexes inherits a weight structure with the restriction of connectives and coconnectives. It is neither left nor right complete. The left and right weight completion
K(\mathrm{Proj}^{\mathrm{fg}}_{\mathbb{Z}})
is given by the full subcategory
\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{Z})^{\mathrm{fg}}\subseteq\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{Z})
of those objects
C
such that
H_{i}(C)
is finitely generated for each
i
.
Another example is obtained by taking the category of compact spectra
\textsf{Sp}^{\omega}
, which is neither left nor right complete. The left completion is given by the category of bounded below spectra with finitely generated homotopy groups (Example
4.22
).

In order to deal with further examples and construct new weight structures, we establish a new general construction principle for weight structures on large categories, which we believe is of independent interest.

Theorem D

(Theorem
6.3
)
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a cocomplete stable category,
\kappa
a regular cardinal,
and
S\subseteq\mathcal{C}^{\kappa}
a small set of
\kappa
-compact objects.
2
2

2

For example any small set of objects in a presentable category.
Then

\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}=\bigcap_{n\geq 1}S[-n]^{\perp}=\{X\in\mathcal{C}\mid\hom(s,X)\text{ is connective for all }s\in S\}

defines a weight structure on
\mathcal{C}
.

Theorem
D
recovers the standard weight structure on spectra by
taking
S=\{\mathbb{S}\}\subseteq\textsf{Sp}
. In this case the weight
connective objects are the connective spectra, and the heart consists
of direct sums of the sphere spectrum. The coconnectives
\textsf{Sp}_{w\leq 0}
are those spectra
X
with
H_{*}(X;\mathbb{Z})=0
for
*>0
and
H_{0}(X;\mathbb{Z})
a free abelian group.

This example already appeared in
work of Bondarko, who proved Theorem
D
under the
additional assumption
\kappa=\omega
. The resulting weight structure on
Sp

is left complete but not right complete as one can easily verify. We will identify its right weight completion as the category of modules over the (spectral) integral Steenrod algebra
A=\hom_{\textsf{Sp}}(\mathbb{Z},\mathbb{Z})
.

Proposition E

(Proposition
10.12
)
.

The functor
\textsf{Sp}\to\textsf{LMod}(A)
that sends a spectrum
X
to its homology
\mathbb{Z}\otimes X
considered as a left module over
A
exhibits the category of left modules
\textsf{LMod}(A)
as the weight completion of
Sp
.
Here
\textsf{LMod}(A)
carries a weight structure in which the connectives are precisely the underlying connectives.

This result clarifies the extent to which a spectrum can be recovered
from its homology, regarded as a highly structured Steenrod module: namely precisely as an object in the right weight completion, and thus bounded below spectra can be completely recovered.
This is reflected in the (integral) Adams spectral
sequence, which computes the map

\hom_{\textsf{Sp}}(X,Y)\longrightarrow\hom_{A}(\mathbb{Z}\otimes X,\,\mathbb{Z}\otimes Y),

whose target admits a Tor-type spectral sequence with
E_{2}
-page

\Ext_{A_{*}}\big(H_{*}(X;\mathbb{Z}),\,H_{*}(Y;\mathbb{Z})\big).

Another interesting weight structure on spectra arises by applying Theorem
6.3
with
S=\{\mathbb{Z}\}\subseteq\textsf{Sp}
(this requires the case
\kappa=\omega_{1}
which was not known before).
In this case the weight coconnective spectra are those spectra whose
positive homotopy groups vanish and whose
\pi_{0}
is free. We refer to this weight structure as the Anderson weight structure, because of the following surprising result:

Proposition F

(Proposition
10.3
, Proposition
10.9
)
.

The weight heart of the Anderson weight structure is given by the subcategory
\{I_{\mathbb{Z}}\}^{\oplus}\subseteq\textsf{Sp}
spanned by arbitrary direct sums of the
Anderson dual of the sphere
I_{\mathbb{Z}}\in\textsf{Sp}
. This weight structure
is right complete but not left complete. The functor

\textsf{Sp}\to\text{{RMod}}(A),\qquad X\mapsto\hom_{\textsf{Sp}}(\mathbb{Z},X)

exhibits the category of right
A
-modules
\text{{RMod}}(A)
as its left weight completion.

We note that the integral Steenrod algebra
A
admits an equivalence

A\simeq A^{\textsf{op}}
induced by Anderson duality. Consequently, there is an equivalence of module categories
\textsf{LMod}(A)\simeq\text{{RMod}}(A)
. It follows that the completions of the two weight structures on spectra (Propositions
E
and
F
) are equivalent.
This should be regarded as the correctly weight-completed refinement of the fact that the functor

\textsf{Sp}\to\textsf{Sp},\qquad X\mapsto I_{\mathbb{Z}}\otimes X

is an equivalence on weight bounded objects (Corollary
10.5
), where the source is equipped with the standard weight structure and the target with the Anderson weight structure.
However, this functor is not weight-exact on its entire domain; while it preserves weight coconnective objects, it does not preserve weight connective ones (Remark
10.7
and Remark
10.14
).

Weak Weight and
t
-completion

The examples discussed above suggest that weight structures arising in practice are often only left complete or only right complete. In many situations, however, a given weight structure is accompanied by a
t
-structure with the same connective objects; such pairs are called
adjacent
. A typical phenomenon is that a category
\mathcal{C}
is left complete with respect to its weight structure and right complete with respect to the adjacent
t
-structure.
This occurs, for instance, for the standard weight structure on spectra.

At first glance, this asymmetry may seem surprising. However, it reflects a fundamental difference in the nature of the two notions of completeness: left weight completeness and right
t
-completeness are governed by colimit conditions, whereas right weight completeness and left
t
-completeness are governed by limit conditions. In practice, stable categories are far more often well-behaved with respect to colimits, or even generated under colimits by a controlled class of objects. This explains why left weight completeness and right
t
-completeness frequently appear together.
Another perspective on this asymmetry is that in the connective part of a
t
-structure,
the Postnikov tower gives a descending filtration, so here completeness should be a limit condition,
whereas in the connective part of a weight structure, weight complexes give ascending filtrations,
where completeness should be a colimit condition.

A natural question is whether such categories can still be recovered from their heart. In the bounded case, this is achieved via the stable envelope. In the present setting, one is led to consider a two-step process: first passing to the left weight completion, and then forming a right
t
-completion. Formally, for an additive category
\mathcal{A}
, we define

\widehat{K}(\mathcal{A})\coloneqq\smash{(K(\mathcal{A})_{>-\infty})}^{\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 7.17496pt$t$\hss}\downarrow}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 7.17496pt$t$\hss}\downarrow}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 7.17496pt$t$\hss}\downarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.5}{$\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 7.17496pt$t$\hss}\downarrow$}}\mkern 12.0mu}}

as the right
t
-completion of
K(\mathcal{A})_{>-\infty}
, which itself is the left weight completion of the stable envelope of
\mathcal{A}
. We also write
\widehat{K}^{\textsf{op}}(\mathcal{A})\coloneqq K(\mathcal{A}^{\textsf{op}})^{\textsf{op}}
which is dual in the sense that it performs right weight completion and left
t
-completion.

At this point, however, a serious difficulty arises. In general, the category
K(\mathcal{A})
need not carry a
t
-structure adjacent to the weight structure, so that
t
-completion is a priori not defined.
One of the key insights of this paper is that this obstruction can be overcome by working in a more flexible framework. We introduce suitable weakenings of
t
-structures and show that the theory of completeness and completion for
t
-structures extends to this setting. In particular,
t
-completion can be carried out in a substantially more general context than previously available, without requiring the existence of finite limits or a genuine adjacent
t
-structure, while retaining its essential formal properties.

To make this precise, we consider weak
t
-structures (and similarly weak weight structures), which still allow for a meaningful notion of completeness and completion.

Definition G

(Definition
7.1
, Remark
7.5
)
.

A weak
t
-structure on a stable category
\mathcal{C}
consists of a full subcategory
\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}
closed under suspensions, retracts, and extensions such that for every
X\in\mathcal{C}
there is a fiber sequence

X^{\prime}\to X\to X^{\prime\prime}

with
X^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}_{>-\infty}
and
X^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{C}_{t\leq 0}\coloneqq(\mathcal{C}_{\geq 1})^{\perp}
.

One advantage of this flexibility is that it allows us, for instance, to define spectrum objects in prestable categories without assuming the existence of finite limits, generalizing the treatment of Lurie in
[
23
]
(see Theorem
8.24
).
In particular, we have
\textsf{Sp}(\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0})\coloneqq\smash{(\mathcal{C}_{>-\infty})}^{\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 7.17496pt$t$\hss}\downarrow}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 7.17496pt$t$\hss}\downarrow}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 7.17496pt$t$\hss}\downarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.5}{$\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 7.17496pt$t$\hss}\downarrow$}}\mkern 12.0mu}},

so that in this language we get an alternative description
\widehat{K}(\mathcal{A})\simeq\textsf{Sp}(\mathcal{P}^{\Delta^{\textsf{op}}}(\mathcal{A}))
.

Similar to weak
t
-structures, we also introduce the notion of weak weight structures (Definition
3.1
), and extend the notions of weight completeness and completion to this setting (i.e. generalize Definition
A
and Theorem
B
). In fact, the entire paper is developed in the generality of weak weight structures. This additional flexibility is essential in practice: for instance, on the perfect derived category of a qcqs scheme
X
, one typically obtains only a weak weight structure (see Examples
3.6
and
6.5
).

Our main theorem shows that categories equipped with compatible weight and
t
-structures can still be recovered from their heart via this generalized completion procedure.

Theorem H

(Theorem
9.9
)
.

A stable
\infty
-category
\mathcal{C}
with a full subcategory
\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}\subseteq\mathcal{C}
closed under finite colimits and extensions is equivalent to
\widehat{K}(\mathcal{C}^{w\heartsuit})
(compatibly with the connectives)
if and only if

(1)

(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0})
is a right complete weak
t
-structure, and

(2)

(\mathcal{C}_{>-\infty},\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0})
is a left complete weight structure.

As a consequence of Theorem
H
, we obtain equivalences expressing familiar stable categories as being built from additive ones:

\textsf{Sp}\simeq\widehat{K}(\mathrm{Proj}_{\mathbb{S}}),\qquad\textsf{Sp}^{\mathrm{fg}}\simeq\widehat{K}(\mathrm{Proj}_{\mathbb{S}}^{\mathrm{fg}}),\qquad\textsf{Sp}\simeq\widehat{K}^{\textsf{op}}(\{I_{\mathbb{Z}}\}^{\oplus}).

More generally, for any connective
\mathbb{E}_{1}
-ring
R
, there is an equivalence

\textsf{LMod}(R)\simeq\widehat{K}(\mathrm{Proj}_{R})

Moreover, if
\mathcal{A}
is a Grothendieck abelian category with enough projectives, then

\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A})\simeq\widehat{K}(\mathcal{A}_{\textsf{proj}})

Finally, we construct a weight structure on the category of Tate objects
\Tate(\mathcal{C})
(in the sense of Hennion
[
18
]
) associated to a weighted category, and give an explicit description of its heart (Theorem
11.3
) as the full subcategory of
\Ind\Pro(\mathcal{C}^{w\heartsuit})
spanned by retracts of objects of the form

\bigoplus_{I}a_{i}\oplus\prod_{J}b_{j}\qquad a_{i},b_{j}\in\mathcal{C}^{w\heartsuit}

generalizing the classical notion of Tate vector spaces.
Under suitable hypotheses on
\mathcal{C}
, we further show that there is a ‘completion’ functor

\Tate(\mathcal{C})\to\widehat{K}\bigl(\Tate(\mathcal{C})^{w\heartsuit})

with the key input being the existence of a weak
t
-structure (Lemma
11.5
and Corollary
11.10
).
As another motivation for the formalism of weak
t
-structures,
we see in Example
11.11
that even in the nice case
\mathcal{C}=\textsf{Sp}^{\omega}
, there exists an adjacent
weak

t
-structure on
\Tate(\textsf{Sp}^{\omega})
, but not an adjacent
t
-structure.
In forthcoming work
[
29
]
, we apply these results on Tate categories to algebraic examples such as
\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{Z})^{\omega}
, and relate these constructions to a full subcategory of condensed
\Ind
-objects in
\mathcal{C}
.

Outline of the Sections

Section
2
recalls the notion of connectivity structure from
[
4
]
and introduces the notions of (co)limit completeness that underlie all the completeness conditions considered in this paper.
Both (weak) weight and (weak)
t
-structures admit underlying stable (co)connectivity structures,
and the various definitions of completeness can already be formulated on this level.
This allows us to give a general theory of functors commuting with certain sequential (co)limits
for (co)connectivity reasons that is crucial for the rest of the paper,
which we highlight via a number of examples.

Section
3
introduces weakly weighted categories, a weakening of the notion of weight structure in which the weight decompositions are allowed to have bounded “defect”.
In the case this defect is bounded by some
0\leq\ell<\infty
,
this coincides up to reformulation with a notion studied by Levy–Sosnilo
[
21
]
.
After developing the basic formalism and introducing the related category
wWCat

of weakly weighted categories and exact functors of bounded weight amplitude,
we show that weight complexes still exist in this generality.

Section
4
is the technical heart of the paper.
We define left weight completeness of weakly weighted categories
\mathcal{C}

and prove Theorem
B
in this generality.
The left completion
\smash{\mathcal{C}}^{\uparrow}
is identified with a canonical full subcategory
of
\Ind(\mathcal{C}_{w<\infty})
,
and we highlight important consequences
such as the existence of a weight complex functor valued in
\Ind
-objects (Corollary
4.10
).

Section
5
defines
K(\mathcal{A})
and proves Corollary
C
.
We show in Theorem
5.5

that the notation is warranted by proving that if
\mathcal{A}
is an (ordinary) additive category,
there is a weight exact equivalence between
K(\mathcal{A})
and an
\infty
-category of chain complexes in
\mathcal{A}

whose homotopy category recovers the classical
K(\mathcal{A})
, one of the motivating examples for weight structures.
Combining this with Theorem
B
(3), we recover in Corollary
5.9

Lurie’s universal property of the bounded below derived category of an abelian category with enough projectives.

Section
6
proves Theorem
D
,
which crucially relies on the results of Appendix
A
.
We apply this to define the standard weight structure on
\textsf{LMod}(R)

for a connective
\mathbb{E}_{1}
-ring
R
and study its completeness properties (Proposition
6.6
).

Section
7
develops the theory of weak
t
-structures and
their right completions, paralleling Sections
3
and

4
.
The main result, Theorem
7.13
, is the direct analogue of
Theorem
B
and recovers the classical right completion
when applied to an ordinary
t
-structure.

Section
8
applies the completion theory to prestable categories.
We define two kinds of colimit completions completions,
one “weight-like” and one “
t
-like”.
The latter lets us define spectrum objects for prestable categories,
and the main result of the section Theorem
8.24

is on the properties of this construction.

Section
9
introduces the functor

\mathcal{A}\mapsto\widehat{K}(\mathcal{A})
on additive categories and proves
Theorem
H
.
We apply this
\textsf{LMod}(R)
for a connective
\mathbb{E}_{1}
-ring
R

and to the derived category
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A})
of a Grothendieck abelian
category with enough projectives (Proposition
9.13
).

Section
10
studies weight structures on
Sp
.
We construct the Anderson weight structure
on spectra mentioned in the introduction
and prove Propositions
E
and
10.9
.
In particular, we show how passing from spectra to modules
over the Steenrod algebra can be seen as a weight completion,
and passing backwards as a
t
-completion.

Section
11
constructs weight structures on Tate categories
under minimal assumptions (Theorem
11.3
).
Moreover, we investigate sufficient conditions
for the existence of adjacent weak
t
-structures (Proposition
11.9
, Corollary
11.10
),
and show that even in very nice examples such as that of finite spectra,
one can in general only expect a
weak
adjacent
t
-structure
(Example
11.11
).

Appendix
A
proves a crucial tool required
for the proof of Theorem
D
.
Concretely, for a given ordinal
\gamma
,
we give concrete conditions
under which the limit of an inverse system of connective spectra
indexed by
\gamma^{\textsf{op}}
is again connective.
For
\gamma=\omega
this recovers the usual Mittag–Leffler criterion.

A.2
, which we learned from him. We also thank Vova Sosnilo for a discussion that led to Proposition
F
. We are grateful to the homotopy theory group in Bielefeld for a joint research seminar, and in particular to Victor Saunier and Fabian Hebestreit for helpful discussions related to weight structures.

Notational Conventions

•

From now on, i.e. in the body of the paper, “category” means “
\infty
-category”.

•

We denote mapping anima by
\map
,
and hom spectra by
\hom
.

•

Given a category
\mathcal{C}
we denote its idempotent completion by
\mathcal{C}^{\textsf{idem}}
.
If
\mathcal{C}
is additive, then
\mathcal{C}^{\textsf{widem}}
denotes its weak idempotent completion,
which is the smallest full subcategory of
\mathcal{C}^{\textsf{idem}}
containing
\mathcal{C}

and closed under cofibers of split monomorphisms.

•

A
t
-category is a stable category with
t
-structure.
A weighted category is a stable category with weight structure.

•

If
\mathcal{C}
is a semiadditive category admitting arbitrary sums,
then for a full subcategory
S\subseteq\mathcal{C}
we denote by
S^{\oplus}\subseteq\mathcal{C}

the full subcategory on sums of objects in
S
.

•

If
\mathcal{C}
is stable and
\mathcal{D}\subseteq\mathcal{C}
a full subcategory, then
we let
{}^{\perp}{\mathcal{D}}\subseteq\mathcal{C}
be the full subcategory on objects
c\in\mathcal{C}

for which all maps
c\to d
for
d\in\mathcal{D}
are nullhomotopic. Dually for
{\mathcal{D}}^{\perp}
.

•

Occasionally, instead of repeating an entire sentence with minimal changes,
we will highlight the alternatives like
(this)
.
For example, we might write “Suppose that
\mathcal{C}
is a category admitting countable coproducts
(countable products)
and pushouts
(pullbacks)
. Then
\mathcal{C}
admits sequential colimits
(sequential limits)
.”

2.
Connectivity structures

The aim of this section is to recall the notion of
connectivity structure from
[
4
]
.
The idea is that this abstracts the notion of connective
maps of spaces to a general category.
The authors of
[
4
]
use this mainly to define a notion of skeleta
and cellular approximations which, although this is not mentioned
by the authors, essentially recovers the notion of weight structures in the setting of stable categories.
Our aim instead is to investigate
how to use the notions of connectivity
to prove that functors preserve certain kinds of (co)limits
for (co)connectivity reasons.
Concretely, we would like to cover the following examples
(which we will reprove below using the developed formalism).

Example 2.1

(Lemmas
2.33
,
2.34
,
2.35
and
2.37
)
.

(1)

For a finite group
G
, the functors
(-)^{hG},(-)_{hG},(-)^{tG}\colon\textsf{Sp}^{BG}\to\textsf{Sp}
preserve the (co)limits of the Whitehead and Postnikov towers.

(2)

If
X\in\textsf{Sp}
is bounded below with finitely generated homotopy (equivalently, homology) groups, then
X\otimes-\colon\textsf{Sp}\to\textsf{Sp}

preserves uniformly bounded below
products, and hence uniformly bounded below sequential limits.
Moreover,
\hom_{\textsf{Sp}}(X,-)\colon\textsf{Sp}\to\textsf{Sp}

preserves uniformly bounded above filtered colimits.

(3)

Suppose
A
is a finite type anima (i.e. a CW complex of finite type),
and that
f\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
is an exact functor of left complete
t
-categories.
Then
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{D}
admit
A
-indexed colimits,
and if
f
restricts to
\mathcal{C}_{t\geq 0}\to\mathcal{D}_{t\geq-n}

for some
n
, then
f
preserves them.

(4)

For
X\in\textsf{An}
we have
\Sigma_{+}^{\infty}X\xrightarrow{\simeq}\lim_{n}\Sigma_{+}^{\infty}\tau_{\leq n}X
in
Sp
.

Let us recall the definition of connectivity structures from
[
4
]
.
Their basic notion is that of
k
-connected maps. In the setting of topoi,
these coincide with
(k+1)
-connective maps, that is, maps whose fibers are

(k+1)
-connective. In what follows, we will instead take connective maps as
the primary notion.

Definition 2.2

(
[
4
, Definition 2.1]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a category admitting pullbacks.
A connectivity structure on
\mathcal{C}
consists
of a collection of
k
-connective maps for every
k\in\mathbb{Z}

satisfying the following axioms:

(1)

k
-connective maps are
(k-1)
-connective.

(2)

Equivalences are
\infty
-connective,
meaning
k
-connective for all
k
.
For convenience, we say that all morphisms
are
-\infty
-connective.

(3)

k
-connective maps are stable under pullback.

(4)

If
f
and
g
are composable morphisms, then:

(a)

If
f
and
g
are
k
-connective, so is
gf
.

(b)

If
gf
is
k
-connective and
f
is
(k-1)
-connective,
then
g
is
k
-connective.

(c)

If
gf
is
k
-connective and
g
is
(k+1)
-connective,
then
f
is
k
-connective.

Given another category
\mathcal{D}
with connectivity structure
we say that a functor
f\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
has amplitude
\geq a

for
-\infty\leq a\leq\infty

if for large enough
k
it sends
(k+a)
-connective maps
to
k
-connective maps.
3
3

3

This is slightly weaker than the notion “adding at least
a
to connectivity” of
[
4
, Definition 2.3]
.

We say
f
has bounded below amplitude if there exists such an
a\in\mathbb{Z}
.
As these are closed under composition,
we obtain a category
\textsf{Cat}^{\textsf{cn}}
of categories with connectivity structure and functors of bounded below amplitude.

Example 2.3

(
[
4
, Example 2.14]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a topos.
Then
\mathcal{X}
admits a connectivity structure
where a map is
k
-connective
precisely if it is
k
-connective in the sense of
[
24
, 6.5.1.10]
.
For
\mathcal{X}=\textsf{An}
,
this recovers the usual notion of connectivity;
a map
f\colon X\to Y
of anima
is
k
-connective precisely if for all choices of basepoints,

\pi_{j}(f)
is an isomorphism for
j<k
and
\pi_{k}(f)
is a surjection.
Equivalently,
f
is
k
-connective precisely if all the fibers are
k
-connective for all choices of basepoints,
where a
0
-connective anima is a nonempty one
and
X\in\textsf{An}
is
k
-connective for
k\geq 1

if
\pi_{\ell}(X)\cong\pi_{\ell}(*)
for
\ell\leq k-1
.

Definition 2.4

(
[
4
, Definition 2.25]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a pointed category equipped with a connectivity
structure. We say an object
X\in\mathcal{C}
is
k
-connective
if the map
X\to*
is
k
-connective.
4
4

4

By
[
4
, Proposition 2.16]
this is equivalent to
*\to X
being
(k-1)
-connective.

We write
\mathcal{C}_{\geq k}\subseteq\mathcal{C}
for the full subcategory of
k
-connective objects, and
\mathcal{C}_{\geq\infty}=\bigcap_{k\geq 0}\mathcal{C}_{\geq k}
.

Definition 2.5

(
[
4
, Definition 2.24]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a stable category.
A connectivity structure on
\mathcal{C}
is stable
if any given map
f
is
k
-connective if and only if
\Omega f
is
(k-1)
-connective.
In particular, we have that
\mathcal{C}_{\geq k}=\{\Sigma^{k}X\mid X\in\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}\}
.

The following Lemma shows that the definition
of a stable connectivity structure simplifies considerably,
essentially being completely determined by
\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}\subseteq\mathcal{C}
.

Lemma 2.6
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a stable category.

(1)

If
\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}\subseteq\mathcal{C}

is a full prestable subcategory in the sense of
[
23
, Appendix C]
, i.e. closed under finite colimits and extensions
5
5

5

Equivalently: contains 0, and is closed under extensions and cofibers.

then this determines a stable connectivity structure
on
\mathcal{C}
by setting
\mathcal{C}_{\geq k}=\{\Sigma^{k}X\mid X\in\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}\}

and defining a map to be
k
-connective if its fiber lies in
\mathcal{C}_{\geq k}
.

(2)

If
\mathcal{C}
is equipped with a stable connectivity structure,
then
\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}\subseteq\mathcal{C}
is closed under finite colimits
and extensions, and
\mathcal{C}_{\geq n}\subseteq\mathcal{C}_{>-\infty}
is closed under retracts.
Moreover, the connectivity structure defined in (1) agrees with the given one on
\mathcal{C}
.

(3)

If
\mathcal{D}
is a prestable category,
then it embeds fully faithfully into its Spanier-Whitehead stabilization

\SW(\mathcal{D})\coloneqq\colim_{n}(\mathcal{D}\xrightarrow{\Sigma}\mathcal{D}\xrightarrow{\Sigma}\mathcal{D}\to\cdots)
(colimit taken in
Cat
),
which is the initial stable category receiving a right exact functor from
\mathcal{D}
.
This determines a stable connectivity structure
\SW(\mathcal{D})_{\geq 0}=\mathcal{D}

which is closed under retracts in
\SW(\mathcal{D})
.

(4)

If
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0})
is a stable connectivity structure,
then the canonical map
\SW(\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0})\to\mathcal{C}
is fully faithful
with essential image
\mathcal{C}_{>-\infty}=\bigcup_{n\geq 0}\mathcal{C}_{\geq-n}
.

Proof.

Except the claim about retracts,
the first two points were noted in
[
4
, Remark 2.30]
.
By shifting and induction, it is enough to show that if
X\in\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}
is a retract of
Y\in\mathcal{C}_{\geq 1}
,
then also
X\in\mathcal{C}_{\geq 1}
. Let
C=\cofib(X\to Y)\in\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}
, so that
Y\simeq X\oplus C
.
Then
X
sits in an extension
Y\to X\to\Sigma C

where the outer terms lie in
\mathcal{C}_{\geq 1}
, so we conclude.
Point (3) follows from (1), (2) and the universal property of
\SW(-)
shown in
[
23
, Proposition C.1.1.7(b)]
.
For (4), we have a cone

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\inc}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega\inc}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{2}\inc}

which induces a comparison functor
f\colon\SW(\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0})\to\mathcal{C}

that clearly has essential image
\mathcal{C}_{>-\infty}
.
Since all the functors in this cone are fully faithful,
it follows that
f
is fully faithful.
∎

Notation 2.7
.

Because of the above Lemma, we will denote a stable connectivity structure by
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0})
.

Remark 2.8
.

If
\mathcal{D}
is a prestable category,
then the connectivity structure on
\SW(\mathcal{D})

induces a good notion of connectivity on
\mathcal{D}

by
\mathcal{D}_{\geq n}=\SW(\mathcal{D})_{\geq n}=\{\Sigma^{n}X\mid X\in\mathcal{D}\}\subseteq\mathcal{D}

for
n\geq 0
, so that
\mathcal{D}_{\geq 0}=\mathcal{D}
.
Since
\mathcal{D}
generally does not admit pullbacks, this is not a connectivity structure in the strict sense
of Definition
2.2
,
but it still makes sense to refer to it as such since
it essentially behaves like one,
and we can perform all arguments in
\SW(\mathcal{D})
where it constitutes a genuine connectivity structure.
We will come back to this in Section
8
.

Convention 2.9
.

From now on, unless specified otherwise, we will always implicitly assume
that a connectivity structure on a stable category is stable.

Remark 2.10
.

By passing to opposite categories,
everything in this section dualizes to a notion of coconnectivity structure
satisfying the dual results.
Concretely, we say that a coconnectivity structure on
\mathcal{C}
is the data
of a connectivity structure on
\mathcal{C}^{\textsf{op}}
.
For example, if
\mathcal{C}
is stable
there is a dual of Lemma
2.6

which says that a coconnectivity structure on
\mathcal{C}

is the same data as a full subcategory
\mathcal{C}_{\leq 0}\subseteq\mathcal{C}

closed under finite limits and extensions.
A functor between stable coconnectivity structures
has amplitude
\leq n
precisely if it restricts to
\mathcal{C}_{\leq 0}\to\mathcal{D}_{\leq n}
.

Observation 2.11
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a category with connectivity structure.

(1)

For any small category
K
,
the category
\textsf{Fun}(K,\mathcal{C})
inherits a pointwise connectivity structure.
Similarly, given
X\in\mathcal{C}
we can declare a map in
\mathcal{C}_{X/}
or
\mathcal{C}_{/X}

to be
k
-connective precisely if it is so in
\mathcal{C}
,
and this defines a connectivity structure on the slices
\mathcal{C}_{/X}
and
\mathcal{C}_{X/}
.

(2)

If
\mathcal{C}
and its connectivity structure
are stable, and
\mathcal{D}
is another such category,
then an exact functor
f\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}

has amplitude
\geq a
if and only if it restricts
to
\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}\to\mathcal{D}_{\geq a}
.

(3)

If
\mathcal{C}
is a stable category with
t
-structure

(\mathcal{C}_{t\geq 0},\mathcal{C}_{t\leq 0})

(weight structure
(\mathcal{C}_{w\geq 0},\mathcal{C}_{w\leq 0})
)

then
\mathcal{C}_{t\geq 0}

(
\mathcal{C}_{w\geq 0}
)

determines a stable connectivity structure on
\mathcal{C}

via Lemma
2.6
.

Definition 2.12
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a category with connectivity structure.
A direct sequential diagram
X_{\bullet}\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathcal{C}

is growing in connectivity if for all
n\geq 0

the map
X_{k}\to X_{k+1}
is
n
-connective for large enough
k
.
Applying this definition to the slice category
\mathcal{C}_{/X}

for some
X
, this defines the notion of a sequential
cone
X_{\bullet}\Rightarrow\const X
growing in connectivity;
namely that for every
n
the map
X_{k}\to X_{k+1}
(equivalently, the map
X_{k}\to X
)
is
n
-connective for large enough
k
.
We also consider the analogously defined notions for inverse sequential diagrams
\mathbb{N}^{\textsf{op}}\to\mathcal{C}
(and their cones).

Observation 2.13
.

If
f\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
is a functor of bounded below amplitude
between categories with connectivity structures,
then composition with
f
preserves sequential diagrams growing in connectivity.

As we will see below (Lemma
2.21
), the point of the definition of these kinds of sequential diagrams
will be that under certain assumptions on the target
\mathcal{D}
a functor of bounded below amplitude
automatically sends sequential cones growing in connectivity to (co)limiting cones.

Example 2.14
.

Endow
An
with the canonical connectivity structure
from Example
2.3
.
The cell structure of a CW complex
X

defines a sequential cone
X_{\bullet}\colon\mathbb{N}\to\textsf{An}_{/X}

growing in connectivity in
An
.
Similarly, if
\mathcal{C}
is a stable category with weight structure
and
X\in\mathcal{C}
, there exists an associated
weight complex
X_{\leq 0}\to X_{\leq 1}\to X_{\leq 2}\to\cdots

over
X
which defines a sequential cone growing in (weight) connectivity
(cf. Lemma
3.18
).

Example 2.15
.

Given
X\in\textsf{An}
, the Postnikov tower
yields an inverse sequential cone
\const X\Rightarrow\tau_{\leq\bullet}X

growing in connectivity.

Example 2.16
.

Let
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0})
be a stable category with connectivity structure
and
X\colon\Delta^{\textsf{op}}\to\mathcal{C}
a simplicial object.
We recall properties of the Dold-Kan correspondence
from
[
22
, Theorem 1.2.4.1, Remarks 1.2.4.2 and 1.2.4.3]

(compare also the prestable version of
[
23
, Theorem C.1.3.1]
):

(1)

There is an equivalence
\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\textsf{op}}}\simeq\mathcal{C}^{\mathbb{N}}
which associates to a simplicial object
X

the sequential diagram
|X|_{0}\to|X|_{1}\to|X|_{2}\to\cdots

where
|X|_{n}\coloneqq\colim_{\Delta^{\textsf{op}}_{\leq n}}X
.

(2)

If the colimits exist, then

|X|\coloneqq\colim_{\Delta^{\textsf{op}}}X\simeq\colim_{n}|X|_{n}
.

(3)

\cofib(|X|_{n}\to|X|_{n+1})
is a direct summand of
\Sigma^{n+1}X_{n+1}
,
hence is
(n+k+1)
-connective whenever
X_{n+1}
is
k
-connective
(since
\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}
is closed under retracts in
\mathcal{C}_{>-\infty}

by Lemma
2.6
).

(4)

In particular, if
X
is uniformly bounded below,
i.e. factors through
\mathcal{C}_{\geq-k}
for some
k
,
then
(|X|_{n})_{n}
is growing in connectivity.

Thus uniformly bounded below geometric realizations are examples
of sequential colimits growing in connectivity.
In fact, point (3) shows that the equivalence in (1)
restricts to an equivalence between
(\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0})^{\Delta^{\textsf{op}}}

and the full subcategory of
(\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0})^{\mathbb{N}}

on sequential diagrams
X_{\bullet}
where
X_{n+1}/X_{n}\in\mathcal{C}_{\geq n+1}

for
n\geq 0
.
We found the description via sequential diagrams to be more convenient for most of our presentation, with the exception of Proposition
4.7
below.

Definition 2.17
.

We say that a connectivity structure on a category
\mathcal{C}
is colimit
(limit)
complete if

(1)

All
\infty
-connective maps are equivalences.

(2)

\mathcal{C}
admits colimits
(limits)
of sequential diagrams growing in connectivity.

(3)

For
k\in\mathbb{Z}
and a sequential diagram growing in connectivity

X_{\bullet}\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathcal{C}

(
X_{\bullet}\colon\mathbb{N}^{\textsf{op}}\to\mathcal{C}
)

all of whose maps are
k
-connective,
also
X_{0}\to\colim_{n}X_{n}

(
\lim_{n}X_{n}\to X_{0}
)
is
k
-connective.
6
6

6

Equivalently,
we can ask that for each
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, the full subcategory of
\Ar(\mathcal{C})
on
k
-connective
maps is closed under colimits
(limits)
of sequential diagrams growing in connectivity
(with respect to the pointwise connectivity structure).

Observation 2.18
.

If
\mathcal{C}
(and its connectivity structure) is stable, the above definition simplifies to:

(1)

\mathcal{C}_{\geq\infty}\coloneqq\bigcap_{n\geq 0}\mathcal{C}_{\geq n}=0
.

(2)

\mathcal{C}
admits sequential colimits
(limits)
growing in connectivity.

(3)

\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}
is closed under sequential colimits
(limits)
growing in connectivity.

Warning 2.19
.

In
[
4
]
, a connectivity structure is called left complete if it satisfies axiom (1), namely that all
\infty
-connective maps are equivalences. In this paper, we will instead refer to this property as “left separated” or “hypercomplete”. Our use of the term completeness is motivated by its compatibility with existing notions in the literature, for instance recovering the notion of a left complete
t
-structure (see Lemma
2.25
below).

Using Example
2.16
we can reformulate (2)+(3)
of Observation
2.18
using geometric realizations.

Lemma 2.20
.

Let
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0})
be a stable connectivity structure.
The following are equivalent

(1)

\mathcal{C}
admits sequential colimits growing in connectivity,
and

(2)

\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}
admits geometric realizations
and
\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}\subseteq\mathcal{C}
preserves them.

Proof.

The implication (1)
\Rightarrow
(2) is immediate
from Example
2.16
.
For the converse, let
X_{\bullet}\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathcal{C}

be growing in connectivity.
By cofinality, we may assume that
X_{n+1}/X_{n}\in\mathcal{C}_{\geq n+1}
for
n\geq 0
.
We need to show that
X_{\bullet}
admits a colimit in
\mathcal{C}

which lies in
\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}
if
X_{\bullet}
is pointwise connective.
By replacing
X_{\bullet}
with
X_{\bullet}/X_{0}

we can assume that
X_{\bullet}
is pointwise connective
and linearly growing in connectivity.
Now Example
2.16
shows that there is some

Y_{\bullet}\in(\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0})^{\Delta^{\textsf{op}}}

whose associated sequential object
under the Dold-Kan correspondence is
X_{\bullet}
.
In particular,
X_{\bullet}
has colimit
|Y_{\bullet}|\in\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}
,
and this is also a colimit in
\mathcal{C}
by assumption.
∎

Lemma 2.21
.

Let
f\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
be a map of categories with connectivity structures
and suppose that
\mathcal{D}
is colimit
(
limit
)
complete.
If
f
has bounded below amplitude,
then it sends direct
(
inverse
)
sequential cones growing in connectivity to colimit
(
limit
)
cones.

Proof.

We consider the colimit complete case, the limit complete one being analogous.
Let
n\geq 0
. It suffices to check that the comparison map
\colim_{k}f(X_{k})\to f(X)
is
n
-connective.
We can write this map as the colimit in
\Ar(\mathcal{D})
of the maps
f(X_{k})\to f(X)
.
Since
f
has bounded below amplitude, by Observation
2.13

this sequential diagram in
\Ar(\mathcal{D})
is still growing in connectivity
and eventually takes values in
n
-connective maps,
so its colimit is
n
-connective by colimit completeness of
\mathcal{D}
.
∎

Corollary 2.22
.

If both
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{D}
are colimit complete
(
limit complete
)
,
then an exact functor of bounded below amplitude
f\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}

preserves colimits
(
limits
)
of sequential diagrams growing in connectivity.

Lemma 2.23
.

(Co)limit completeness is invariant under equivalence in
\textsf{Cat}^{\textsf{cn}}
.
Concretely, let
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{D}
be categories with connectivity structures
and
F\colon\mathcal{C}\simeq\mathcal{D}
an equivalence so that
F
and
F^{-1}
have bounded below amplitude.
Then
\mathcal{C}
is (co)limit complete if and only if
\mathcal{D}
is.

Remark 2.24
.

In particular, if
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0})
and
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{\geq 0})

are two stable connectivity structures on
\mathcal{C}
so that
\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}\subseteq\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{\geq-a}\subseteq\mathcal{C}_{\geq-(a+b)}
for some
a,b\in\mathbb{Z}
, then
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0})
is (co)limit complete if and only if
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{\geq 0})
is.

Proof.

Suppose that
\mathcal{D}
is colimit complete (the limit case being similar).
If
f
is an
\infty
-connective map in
\mathcal{C}
, then
Ff
is
\infty
-connective in
\mathcal{D}
,
hence an equivalence. Since
F
is conservative, also
f
is an equivalence.
If
X_{\bullet}\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathcal{C}
is a sequential diagram growing in connectivity,
then so is
FX_{\bullet}
, and hence the latter admits colimit in
\mathcal{D}
.
But then one checks that
F^{-1}(\colim_{n}FX_{n})
is also the colimit of
X_{\bullet}
in
\mathcal{C}
.
Finally, let
F
resp.
F^{-1}
have amplitude
\geq-a
resp.
\geq-b

and
X_{\bullet}\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathcal{C}
be a sequential diagram growing in connectivity
where all maps are connective (the same argument works for
k
-connective maps).
Let
X=\colim_{n}X_{n}
be the colimit.
Now pick
n
large enough so that all maps
X_{k}\to X_{k+1}
are
(a+b)
-connective for
k\geq n
.
Using colimit completeness of
\mathcal{D}
, we see that
FX_{n}\to\colim_{k\geq n}FX_{k}
is
b
-connective,
and hence
X_{n}\to X
is connective. Since also
X_{0}\to X_{n}
is connective,
we deduce that
X_{0}\to X
is connective, as desired.
∎

Lemma 2.25
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a stable category with
t
-structure.
The following are equivalent:

(1)

The
t
-structure on
\mathcal{C}
is left complete.

(2)

The connectivity structure
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}_{t\geq 0})
is limit complete.

(3)

The connectivity structure
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}_{t\geq 0})
is both limit and colimit complete.

Remark 2.26
.

As the proof shows, this crucially relies on the functoriality
of truncations in
t
-structures, and therefore
has no direct weight analogue.

Proof.

Clearly (3) implies (2), and the implication (2)
\Rightarrow
(1)
follows from
[
22
, Prop. 1.2.1.19]
,
noting that by the discussion above Proposition 1.2.1.17 of op. cit.,
the proof actually only needs the existence
of sequential limits growing in connectivity
and the closure of
\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}
under them,
as opposed to general countable products.
Specifically, we can identify
\widehat{\mathcal{C}}\coloneqq\lim_{n}\mathcal{C}_{\leq n}

with the full subcategory of
\textsf{Fun}(\mathbb{N}^{\textsf{op}},\mathcal{C})
on those diagrams

(X_{n})_{n}
where
X_{n}\in\mathcal{C}_{\leq n}
and
X_{n+1}\to X_{n}

induces an equivalence on
\tau_{\leq n}
.
Note that such sequential diagrams are in particular growing in connectivity,
so limit completeness guarantees that the canonical map

\mathcal{C}\to\widehat{\mathcal{C}}
admits a right adjoint given by taking the limit.
We want to show that unit and counit of this adjunction form an equivalence.
The counit at an object
(X_{n})_{n}\in\widehat{\mathcal{C}}
is given by the map

\tau_{\leq n}\lim_{k}X_{k}\to X_{n}
in the
n
-th component.
To see that this is an equivalence, we show that
\fib(\lim_{k}X_{k}\to X_{n})=\lim_{k\geq n}\fib(X_{k}\to X_{n})
lies in
\mathcal{C}_{\geq n+1}
.
But
(\fib(X_{k}\to X_{n}))_{k\geq n}
is a sequential diagram growing
in connectivity and taking values in
\mathcal{C}_{\geq n+1}
, so this follows
from limit completeness. Thus the counit is an equivalence.
The unit
X\to\lim_{n}\tau_{\leq n}X
is also clearly an equivalence,
since by similar arguments its fiber lies in
\mathcal{C}_{\geq\infty}=0
.
This concludes the proof of (2)
\Rightarrow
(1).

It remains to show that (1) implies (3).
If
X\in\mathcal{C}_{\geq\infty}
, then
\tau_{\leq n}X=0
for all
n
, and hence
X=0
by left completeness.
Next we show that
\mathcal{C}
admits limits and colimits of sequential diagrams growing in connectivity.
By left completeness, the functors
\tau_{\leq n}\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}_{\leq n}

respectively
\tau_{\leq n}\colon\mathcal{C}_{\leq n+1}\to\mathcal{C}_{\leq n}

induce an equivalence
\mathcal{C}\simeq\lim_{n}\mathcal{C}_{\leq n}
.

The crucial point now is that given a sequential diagram
X_{\bullet}\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathcal{C}

(analogously for
\mathbb{N}^{\textsf{op}}\to\mathcal{C}
) growing in connectivity,
each
\tau_{\leq n}X_{\bullet}
is eventually constant, so admits a (co)limit.
Moreover, any functor preserves (co)limits of eventually constant sequential diagrams.
It follows that
\lim_{n}\mathcal{C}_{\leq n}\simeq\mathcal{C}
admits (co)limits of
sequential diagrams growing in connectivity,
see e.g.
[
25
,
Tag 06B5
]
.

Finally, we need to argue that the connectives are closed under such (co)limits.
As in any
t
-structure, the connectives are closed under all existing colimits,
so consider
X_{\bullet}\colon\mathbb{N}^{\textsf{op}}\to\mathcal{C}
growing in connectivity.
Up to cofinality, we may assume that
\fib(X_{n+1}\to X_{n})\in\mathcal{C}_{\geq n+1}
.
Hence
X_{n+1}\to X_{n}
is inverted by
\tau_{\leq n}
,
and we see that
\lim_{k}\tau_{\leq n}X_{k}\simeq\tau_{\leq n}X_{n}
.
Under the equivalence
\mathcal{C}\simeq\lim_{n}\mathcal{C}_{\leq n}
, we can thus identify the limit of
X_{\bullet}

with the tower
(\tau_{\leq n}X_{n})_{n}
with transition maps
\tau_{\leq n+1}X_{n+1}\to\tau_{\leq n}X_{n+1}\simeq\tau_{\leq n}X_{n}
.
In other words, this tower identifies with the Postnikov tower of
\lim X_{\bullet}
.
From this description it is clear that if
X_{\bullet}
is pointwise connective,
then also
\tau_{\leq 0}(\lim X_{\bullet})\simeq\tau_{\leq 0}X_{0}
is connective, and hence
\lim X_{\bullet}
is too.
∎

Warning 2.27
.

If
\mathcal{C}
is stable with
t
-structure
whose underlying stable connectivity structure is colimit complete,
then it does not have to be left complete.
Indeed, if
\mathcal{C}
admits sequential colimits growing in connectivity
and the
t
-structure is left separated (so that
\mathcal{C}_{\geq\infty}=0
),
then it is already colimit complete since for any
t
-structure
the connectives are closed under all colimits.
A concrete example of a stable cocomplete category
with left separated but not left complete
t
-structure
is
\mathcal{C}=\Shv^{\textsf{hyp}}(X;\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{Z}))
where
X=\prod_{n\geq 1}S^{n}
,
which we learned from
[
15
, Section 2.3]
.
The canonical
t
-structure is left separated by hypercompleteness,
and clearly we have all colimits.
However for
\mathcal{F}=\bigoplus_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{Z}[n]

(where
\mathbb{Z}[n]
is the constant sheaf on
\mathbb{Z}[n]\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{Z})
)
we have that
\mathcal{F}\to\lim_{n}\tau_{\leq n}\mathcal{F}=\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{Z}[n]

is not an equivalence by
[
15
, Examples 2.15,2.16]
.

Example 2.28
.

The
t
-structure on
Sp
is complete,
and hence
(\textsf{Sp},\textsf{Sp}_{\geq 0})
is both limit and colimit complete.

Example 2.29
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a topos, and equip it with the connectivity
structure from Example
2.3
.
Analogously to Lemma
2.25

one can show that the following are equivalent:

(1)

Postnikov towers converge in
\mathcal{X}
.

(2)

The connectivity structure on
\mathcal{X}
is limit complete.

(3)

The connectivity structure on
\mathcal{X}
is limit and colimit complete.

We warn the reader that it does not seem to be known
whether generally, convergence of Postnikov towers implies
Postnikov-completeness of topoi, cf.
[
15
, Warning 1.23]
.
In the following, we will only use that the canonical connectivity
structure on
An
is limit complete, which also easily follows
from the Milnor sequence on homotopy groups,
so we leave the proof of the above equivalences to the interested reader.

As a next step, we want to show that colimit completeness
implies a certain idempotent completeness in the setting
of stable connectivity structures.
To prove this, we will need a strengthening
of
[
22
, Proposition 1.3.3.10(1)]
,
which says that a category admitting finite coproducts
and geometric realizations admits all coproducts.

Lemma 2.30
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a category admitting finite coproducts
and geometric realizations.
If
K
is a quasi-category such that each set of
n
-simplices
K_{n}
is finite
then
\mathcal{C}
admits
K
-indexed colimits.
More generally, if
\mathcal{D}
is a (
\infty
-)category so that
\map(\Delta^{n},\mathcal{D})\in\textsf{An}^{\omega}

for all
n\geq 0
, then
\mathcal{C}
admits
\mathcal{D}
-indexed colimits.
In particular,
\mathcal{C}
is idempotent complete.

Proof.

The classical “coproduct version” of the Bousfield-Kan formula (see
[
33
, Corollary 12.3]
)
immediately shows the statement for
K
-indexed colimits.
In this way we also recover
[
22
, Proposition 1.3.3.10(1)]
,
that
\mathcal{C}
admits finite colimits (since it only remains to check the existence of pushouts).

Moreover, once we know that
\mathcal{C}
is idempotent complete,
it follows from
[
23
, Lemma 21.1.2.14]
that if
\mathcal{C}
admits
K
-indexed
colimits and
L
is a retract of
K
, then
\mathcal{C}
admits
L
-indexed colimits.
In particular, it will follow that
\mathcal{C}
admits colimits indexed by compact (as opposed to only finite)
anima, and then we can conclude via the
\infty
-categorical Bousfield-Kan formula (see
[
33
, Corollary 12.5]
),
which precisely shows that if
\mathcal{C}
admits geometric realizations and
\map(\Delta^{n},\mathcal{D})
-indexed
colimits for all
n\geq 0
, then it also admits
\mathcal{D}
-indexed colimits (and provides a formula for this colimit).

Thus it remains to show idempotent completeness of
\mathcal{C}
.
Now
\textsf{Idem}=N(\mathrm{Idem})
is the nerve of the 1-category
\mathrm{Idem}

which has one object
x
and two endomorphisms
\id_{x}
and
e
, subject to the relation
e^{2}=e
.
The nerve
Idem
has a particularly simple description;
it is the simplicial set obtained from the terminal semi-simplicial set by freely adjoining degeneracies.
Concretely, for each
n\geq 0
, the set of
n
-simplices is in bijection with the set of surjective,
order-preserving maps

d\colon[n]\twoheadrightarrow[m]

in
\Delta
. In particular, the set of
n
-simplices in this nerve is finite for all
n
,
and we conclude by applying the first part.

7
7

7

Given
\textsf{Idem}\to\mathcal{C}
classifying an idempotent endomorphism

e\colon X\to X
, the (cofinal) semi-simplicial subobject of the simplicial object
provided by the Bousfield-Kan formula looks as follows: each object is
X
,
the first face map is always
e
, and all other face maps are identities.
The colimit of this (semi-)simplicial diagram is the retract classified by
e
.

∎

Corollary 2.31
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a prestable category (e.g. the connective
part of a stable connectivity structure).
Then
\mathcal{C}
admits geometric realizations if and only if it admits
sequential colimits growing in connectivity (with respect
to the canonical notion of connectivity of Remark
2.8
).
In this case,
\mathcal{C}
and hence also
\SW(\mathcal{C})
are idempotent complete.
In particular, if
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0})
is a colimit complete stable connectivity structure,
then
\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}
and
\mathcal{C}_{>-\infty}
are idempotent complete.

Proof.

This follows immediately
from Lemma
2.30

and the prestable Dold-Kan correspondence of
[
23
, Theorem C.1.3.1]
,
which allows us to view geometric realizations
as sequential colimits growing in connectivity
analogously to the stable case we recalled in Example
2.16
.
∎

If
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0})
is a limit complete stable connectivity structure,
there is generally no reason for it to admit totalizations (or geometric realizations).
However, the idempotent completeness still holds.

Lemma 2.32
.

Let
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0})
be a limit complete stable connectivity structure.
Then
\SW(\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0})\simeq\mathcal{C}_{>-\infty}
and
\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}
are idempotent complete.

Proof.

This is analogous to the proof of
[
3
, Lemma 2.4]
.
By Thomason’s theorem
[
37
]
the zeroth (connective) algebraic
K
-theory functor

K_{0}\colon\textsf{Cat}_{\textsf{st}}\to\textsf{Ab}
induces for any given stable category
\mathcal{D}
a bijection

\{\text{dense stable subcategories }\mathcal{D}_{0}\subseteq\mathcal{D}\}\leftrightarrow\{\text{subgroups }H\leq K_{0}(\mathcal{D})\}.

In particular, if we can show that
K_{0}(\mathcal{D}^{\textsf{idem}})=0
, then it follows that
\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{D}^{\textsf{idem}}
.
So let
\mathcal{D}=\SW(\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0})\simeq\mathcal{C}_{>-\infty}
.
We establish that
K_{0}(\mathcal{D}^{\textsf{idem}})=0
by an Eilenberg Swindle.
Note that if
X\in\mathcal{C}_{>-\infty}
, then
\cdots\to\Sigma^{4}X\oplus\Sigma^{2}X\oplus X\to\Sigma^{2}X\oplus X\to X

is a sequential diagram growing in connectivity, so its limit
\prod_{n\geq 0}\Sigma^{2n}X
exists.
This defines an endofunctor
S\colon\mathcal{D}\to\mathcal{D},\ X\mapsto\prod_{n\geq 0}\Sigma^{2n}X
.
Note also that
S\simeq\Sigma^{2}S\oplus\id
.
Now
S
extends to an endofunctor on idempotent completions
S^{\prime}\colon\mathcal{D}^{\textsf{idem}}\to\mathcal{D}^{\textsf{idem}}

which still satisfies
S^{\prime}\simeq\Sigma^{2}S^{\prime}\oplus\id
.
Since
\Sigma^{2}
induces the identity on
K_{0}
, it follows that
K_{0}(S^{\prime})\oplus K_{0}(\id)=K_{0}(S^{\prime})
,
and hence that
K_{0}(\id)=0
, i.e.
K_{0}(\mathcal{D}^{\textsf{idem}})=0
, as desired.
Thus
\mathcal{D}
is idempotent complete.
The same follows for
\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}
as it is closed under retracts in
\mathcal{C}_{>-\infty}
by Lemma
2.6
.
∎

We now discuss the examples from the introduction of this section.
They are well known to experts, but we want to highlight
how they are easily intuited and proved via the formalism developed here.

Lemma 2.33
.

For a finite group
G
, the functors
(-)^{hG},(-)_{hG},(-)^{tG}\colon\textsf{Sp}^{BG}\to\textsf{Sp}
preserve the (co)limits of the Whitehead and Postnikov towers.

Proof.

The category
Sp
is presentable stable with complete
t
-structure,
so the underlying (stable) connectivity structure is both limit and colimit complete (Lemma
2.25
).
Clearly
(-)_{hG}
preserves colimits and
(-)^{hG}
preserves limits,
so due to the canonical fiber sequence
(-)_{hG}\to(-)^{hG}\to(-)^{tG}

it suffices to prove that
(-)_{hG}
preserves sequential limits growing in (
t
-)connectivity
and
(-)^{hG}
preserves sequential colimits growing in
t
-coconnectivity,
where we equip
\textsf{Sp}^{BG}
with the pointwise
t
-structure.
Since
\textsf{Sp}_{\geq 0}
is closed under colimits,
(-)_{hG}
has amplitude
\geq 0
,
and thus preserves such limits by Corollary
2.22
.
Dually (cf. Remark
2.10
)
(-)^{hG}
has coconnectivity amplitude
\leq 0
, so preserves such colimits.
∎

Lemma 2.34
.

Let
X\in\textsf{Sp}
be bounded below with finitely generated
homotopy (equivalently, homology) groups.
Then:

(1)

X\otimes-\colon\textsf{Sp}\to\textsf{Sp}

preserves uniformly bounded below products
and hence uniformly bounded below sequential limits.
For example, this shows
\mathbb{S}_{p}^{\land}\otimes\mathbb{Z}\simeq\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\land}
.

(2)

\hom_{\textsf{Sp}}(X,-)\colon\textsf{Sp}\to\textsf{Sp}

preserves uniformly bounded above filtered colimits.

Proof.

We only prove (1), the argument for point (2) being analogous.
Recall that the
t
-structure on
Sp
is compatible with the monoidal structure,
so that
\textsf{Sp}_{\geq 0}
is closed under tensor products.
Moreover, products and coproducts / filtered colimits are
t
-exact.
It follows that
\lim\colon\textsf{Sp}^{\mathbb{N}^{\textsf{op}}}\to\textsf{Sp}
has amplitude
\geq-1
;
a sequential inverse limit of connective spectra is
(-1)
-connective.
Now let
X\in\textsf{Sp}_{\geq 0}^{\textsf{fg}}
be a connective spectrum
with finitely generated homology groups.
It is well known that then there exists a cell structure
X=\colim_{n}X_{n}

where each
X_{n}
is finite and each
\cofib(X_{n}\to X_{n+1})

is a finite sum of
\Sigma^{n+1}\mathbb{S}
,
so that in particular
X_{\bullet}
is growing in connectivity
(for example, this follows from the weight structure on
\textsf{Sp}^{\textsf{fg}}_{>-\infty}

which is restricted from the standard weight structure on
Sp
).
We show that
X\otimes-
preserves uniformly bounded below products.
Indeed, for a collection of (without loss of generality)
connective spectra
(Y_{i})_{i\in I}
we have a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X\otimes\prod_{i}Y_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\prod_{i}X\otimes Y_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\colim_{n}X_{n}\otimes\prod_{i}Y_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\colim_{n}\prod_{i}X_{n}\otimes Y_{i}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

where in the bottom we use that each
X_{n}
is finite. Finally, products are
t
-exact,
so
\prod_{i}\colon\textsf{Sp}^{I}\to\textsf{Sp}
has bounded amplitude,
and
(X_{\bullet}\otimes Y_{i})_{i}
is a sequential diagram in
\textsf{Sp}^{I}
growing in connectivity,
so that its colimit is preserved by
\prod_{i}
.
So the remaining two maps in the square are equivalences, as desired.
∎

Lemma 2.35
.

Suppose
A
is a finite type anima (i.e. a CW complex of finite type),
and that
f\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
is an exact functor of left complete
t
-categories.
Then
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{D}
admit colimits of uniformly bounded below
A
-indexed diagrams,
and if
f
restricts to
\mathcal{C}_{t\geq 0}\to\mathcal{D}_{t\geq-n}

for some
n
, then
f
preserves them.
Dually, if
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{D}
are right
t
-complete
then they admit limits of uniformly bounded above
A
-indexed diagrams
and functors of bounded above
t
-amplitude preserve them.

Proof.

Let
A\in\textsf{An}
be some space, and suppose we have a cell structure
A=\colim_{n}A_{n}

where
A_{0}
is a set and
A_{n}\to A_{n+1}
is obtained by attaching
(n+1)
-cells,
i.e. we have pushouts

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\coprod_{i\in I_{n+1}}S^{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\coprod_{i\in I_{n+1}}*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A_{n+1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\ell_{i})_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(a_{i})_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

Now let
\mathcal{C}
be a stable category with
t
-structure and enough (co)limits
and
\varphi\colon A\to\mathcal{C}
be some diagram. Then we have the following
pushout / pullback squares in
\mathcal{C}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bigoplus_{i}\colim_{S^{n}}\varphi\ell_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\colim_{A_{n}}\varphi|_{A_{n}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\prod_{i}\Omega^{n+1}\varphi(a_{i})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lim_{A_{n+1}}\varphi|_{A_{n+1}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\prod_{i}\varphi(a_{i})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bigoplus_{i}\varphi(a_{i})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\colim_{A_{n+1}}\varphi|_{A_{n+1}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bigoplus_{i}\Sigma^{n+1}\varphi(a_{i})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lim_{A_{n}}\varphi|_{A_{n}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\prod_{i}\lim_{S^{n}}\varphi\ell_{i}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

Note also that generally
\colim_{n}\colim_{A_{n}}\varphi|_{A_{n}}\simeq\colim_{A}\varphi

and analogously for limits. We thus make the following observations:

(1)

If
\varphi
takes uniformly bounded below values, then

\colim_{A_{0}}\varphi\to\colim_{A_{1}}\varphi\to\colim_{A_{2}}\varphi\to\cdots

is a sequential diagram growing in
t
-connectivity.

(2)

If
\varphi
takes uniformly bounded above values, then

\cdots\to\lim_{A_{2}}\varphi\to\lim_{A_{1}}\varphi\to\lim_{A_{0}}\varphi

is a sequential diagram growing in
t
-coconnectivity.

Now assume that
A
is of finite type,
so that each
A_{n}
is a finite
8
8

8

In fact, as we argued in the proof of Lemma
2.30
,
A_{n}
being compact is enough here.

anima.
If
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{D}
are stable categories with left complete

t
-structure, then by Lemma
2.25

they are colimit complete, and thus admit colimits of uniformly bounded below

A
-indexed diagrams by the above analysis.
Moreover, if
f\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
is an exact functor restricting
to
\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}\to\mathcal{D}_{\geq-n}
for some
n
,
then
f
has bounded amplitude and thus preserves such colimits.
Dually, if
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{D}
are right
t
-complete,
they admit
A
-indexed limits of uniformly bounded above diagrams
by the dual of Lemma
2.25
,
and if
f\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
is an exact functor
which restricts to
\mathcal{C}_{\leq 0}\to\mathcal{D}_{\leq n}
for some
n
,
then it preserves those limits.
∎

Corollary 2.36
.

The
t
-structure on
Sp
is complete and compatible with filtered colimits and products,
so that if
I
is a filtered category and
J
a set
the functors
\colim\colon\textsf{Sp}^{I}\to\textsf{Sp}
and
\prod_{J}\colon\textsf{Sp}^{J}\to\textsf{Sp}

are
t
-exact for the pointwise
t
-structures.
The above shows that if
A
is a finite type anima,
then these functors preserve colimits resp. limits of uniformly bounded below resp. above
A
-indexed diagrams.
Equivalently,
\colim_{A}\colon\textsf{Sp}^{A}\to\textsf{Sp}
preserves uniformly bounded below products,
and
\lim_{A}\colon\textsf{Sp}^{A}\to\textsf{Sp}
preserves uniformly bounded above filtered colimits.

Lemma 2.37
.

For
X\in\textsf{An}
we have
\Sigma_{+}^{\infty}X\xrightarrow{\simeq}\lim_{n}\Sigma_{+}^{\infty}\tau_{\leq n}X
in
Sp
.

Proof.

The functor
\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}\colon\textsf{An}\to\textsf{Sp}
is a functor
of bounded below amplitude between limit complete connectivity structures
(cf. Example
2.3
).
Thus it preserves sequential inverse limits growing in connectivity,
of which Postnikov towers are the canonical example.
∎

3.
Weak weight structures

It turns out that the theory of completions of weight structures developed in this paper—specifically in Section
4
- also extends to a natural weakening of the definition of weight structures. In most cases, this weaker notion coincides with one considered by Levy-Sosnilo in
[
21
]
.
In this section, we begin by introducing this variant,
and then show in subsection
3.1
, as in the setting of weighted categories,
that weight complexes still exist.

Definition 3.1
.

Let
\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{0}\cup\{\infty\}
.
A weakly
\ell
-weighted category is a stable category
\mathcal{C}

together with full subcategories
\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0},\mathcal{C}_{\leq 0}\subseteq\mathcal{C}
such that

(1)

\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}
is closed under suspensions,

\mathcal{C}_{\leq 0}
under loops, and both under extensions and retracts.
9
9

9

In particular,
\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}
is closed under finite colimits and
\mathcal{C}_{\leq 0}
under finite limits.

(2)

\hom_{\mathcal{C}}(X_{\leq 0},Y_{\geq 0})\in\textsf{Sp}_{\geq 0}

for
X_{\leq 0}\in\mathcal{C}_{\leq 0}
and
Y_{\geq 0}\in\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}
.

(3)

For every
X\in\mathcal{C}
there is a cofiber sequence

X_{<\ell}\to X\to X_{\geq 0}

with
X_{<\ell}\in\mathcal{C}_{<\ell}
and
X_{\geq 0}\in\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}
.
We refer to this as a weight decomposition of
X
.

Here
\mathcal{C}_{\geq n}=\Sigma^{n}\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}
and
\mathcal{C}_{<n+1}=\mathcal{C}_{\leq n}=\Sigma^{n}\mathcal{C}_{\leq 0}

for
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, and
\mathcal{C}_{<\infty}=\bigcup_{n\geq 0}\mathcal{C}_{\leq n}
. In the case
\ell=\infty
, we simply refer to such a structure as weakly weighted.

Warning 3.2
.

Suppose that
(\mathcal{C}^{\textsf{op}},(\mathcal{C}_{\leq 0})^{\textsf{op}},(\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0})^{\textsf{op}})
is weakly
\ell
-weighted.
For
\ell<\infty
,
it is straightforward to check that this is the same
as
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0},\mathcal{C}_{\leq 0})
being weakly
\ell
-weighted.
However for
\ell=\infty
, the decomposition axiom (3)
slightly changes to require the existence of decompositions

X_{\leq 0}\to X\to X_{>-\infty}
in
\mathcal{C}
.
In this case, we call
\mathcal{C}
weakly co-weighted,
and weakly bi-weighted if both
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0},\mathcal{C}_{\leq 0})

and
(\mathcal{C}^{\textsf{op}},(\mathcal{C}_{\leq 0})^{\textsf{op}},(\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0})^{\textsf{op}})
are weakly weighted.

Note that if
0\leq\ell\leq\ell^{\prime}\leq\infty
, then any weakly
\ell
-weighted structure
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0},\mathcal{C}_{\leq 0})
is also weakly
\ell^{\prime}
-weighted. In particular, every weakly
\ell
-weighted category is weakly weighted.

Remark 3.3
.

Suppose that
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0},\mathcal{C}_{\leq 0})
satisfies axioms (1) and (2) of Definition
3.1
.
If
\mathcal{C}_{<\infty}=\mathcal{C}
, then
\mathcal{C}
is weakly weighted via the trivial decompositions
X\xrightarrow{\id}X\to 0
for all
X\in\mathcal{C}
.
Dually, if
\mathcal{C}_{>-\infty}=\mathcal{C}
, then
\mathcal{C}
is weakly co-weighted via the trivial decompositions
0\to X\xrightarrow{\id}X
.
So if
\mathcal{C}_{<\infty}=\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{C}_{>-\infty}
, then
\mathcal{C}
is weakly bi-weighted.
In particular, if
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0})
is a stable connectivity structure
we can define
\mathcal{C}_{\leq 0}={}^{\perp}{(\mathcal{C}_{\geq 1})}
.
Then
(\mathcal{C}_{>-\infty},\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0},\mathcal{C}_{(-\infty,0])})
is always weakly co-weighted
since
\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}\subseteq\mathcal{C}_{>-\infty}
is closed under retracts (Lemma
2.6
),
and
(\mathcal{C}_{<\infty},\mathcal{C}_{[0,\infty)},\mathcal{C}_{\leq 0})
is weakly weighted
if
\mathcal{C}_{[0,\infty)}\subseteq\mathcal{C}_{<\infty}
is closed under retracts.

In this sense, the notions of weakly
\infty
-(co-)weighted categories and their associated decompositions are only genuinely interesting for unbounded objects. Nevertheless, even when the original category
\mathcal{C}
is bounded, its completions studied in the next section remain nontrivial and worthy of study. Thus, the observation may also be interpreted as saying that the bounded case fits into the completion framework without requiring any additional hypotheses.

Remark 3.4
.

A priori, there is another way to reasonably weaken the axioms
of a weighted category;
for
n,\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, let us temporarily define an
(n,\ell)
-weighted category
\mathcal{C}

as a stable category
\mathcal{C}
with two full subcategories

\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0},\mathcal{C}_{\leq 0}
still satisfying axioms (1) and (3) as above,
but with the following instead of (2):

(2’)

\hom_{\mathcal{C}}(X_{\leq 0},Y_{\geq 0})\in\textsf{Sp}_{\geq-n}

for
X_{\leq 0}\in\mathcal{C}_{\leq 0}
and
Y_{\geq 0}\in\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}
.

Given an
(n,\ell)
-weighted category
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0},\mathcal{C}_{\leq 0})
,
it is straightforward from the definitions to show that

•

(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}_{\geq k},\mathcal{C}_{\leq k})
is
(n,\ell)
-weighted for all
k\in\mathbb{Z}
.

•

(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0},\mathcal{C}_{\leq-n})
is
(0,n+\ell)
-weighted.

•

(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}_{\geq-\ell},\mathcal{C}_{\leq 0})
is
(n+\ell,0)
-weighted.

In
[
21
]
the
(\ell,0)
-weighted categories which are bounded
(i.e.
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{C}_{<\infty}=\mathcal{C}_{>-\infty}
)
were called heart categories of cohomological dimension
\leq\ell
.
10
10

10

A priori, their definition does not include that
\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}
and
\mathcal{C}_{\leq 0}
are closed under retracts in
\mathcal{C}
. However, given the other assumptions, this actually follows from boundedness of
\mathcal{C}
, cf. Lemma
2.6
.

In particular, this notion is structurally equivalent
to that of
(0,\ell)
-weighted categories,
i.e. our notion of weakly
\ell
-weighted categories,
but we will adopt the latter formulation for our purposes.

Example 3.5
.

In view of the above Remark,
[
21
, Example 2.18]
shows that if
R
is any
(-1)
-connective
ring spectrum, then
\Perf(R)
is weakly
1
-weighted
for
\Perf(R)_{\leq 0}
resp.
\Perf(R)_{\geq 0}
the retract closures of the extension closures
of the categories
\{\Sigma^{-n}R\mid n\geq 0\}
resp.
\{\Sigma^{n}R\mid n\geq 1\}
. Note that we need
n\geq 1
in the connectives due to the shift in translating from weakly
(1,0)
-weighted
to weakly
(0,1)
-weighted category in the notation of Remark
3.4
.
More generally, we can consider a
(-\ell)
-connective ring spectrum
R
for some
0\leq\ell<\infty

and define
\Perf(R)_{\leq 0}
as before and
\Perf(R)_{\geq 0}
as the retract closure
of the extension closure of
\{\Sigma^{n}R\mid n\geq\ell\}
.
Then
(\Perf(R),\Perf(R)_{\geq 0},\Perf(R)_{\leq 0})
satisfies axioms (1) and (2)
of Definition
3.1
,
however by
[
21
, Remark 2.19]
it is generally not weakly
\ell
-weighted.
On the other hand, it is trivially weakly
\infty
-(co-)weighted by Remark
3.3

since
\Perf(R)=\Perf(R)_{<\infty}=\Perf(R)_{>-\infty}
.

Example 3.6
.

Let
X
be a qcqs scheme and
\Perf(X)
its stable category of perfect complexes.
Then there exists some
\ell<\infty
so that
\Perf(X)
is weakly
\ell
-weighted,
with
\Perf(X)_{\leq 0}
consisting of the perfect complexes of Tor-amplitude
\leq 0
,
and
\Perf(X)_{\geq 0}=(\Perf(X)_{\leq-1})^{\perp}
.
We will come back to and prove this in Section
6
below,
see Example
6.5
.

Definition 3.7
.

An exact functor
f\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
between weakly weighted categories
has (weight) amplitude
[a,b]
for
-\infty\leq a\leq b\leq\infty

if the underlying functor of connectivity structures
has connectivity amplitude
\geq a
and coconnectivity amplitude
\leq b
.
Equivalently (cf. Observation
2.11
(2)),
if it restricts to
\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}\to\mathcal{D}_{\geq a}
and
\mathcal{C}_{\leq 0}\to\mathcal{D}_{\leq b}
.
Here
\mathcal{D}_{\geq-\infty}=\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{D}_{\leq\infty}
.
Let us also say that
f
has amplitude in
(a,b)

if it restricts to
\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}\to\mathcal{C}_{>a}
and
\mathcal{C}_{\leq 0}\to\mathcal{C}_{<b}

and similarly for amplitude
[a,b)
or
(a,b]
.
We say that
f
has bounded below (weight) amplitude if
a\in\mathbb{Z}
,
and bounded amplitude if
a,b\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Definition 3.8
.

Given two weakly weighted categories
\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D}
and
-\infty\leq a\leq b\leq\infty
we let

\textsf{Fun}^{\textsf{ex}}_{[a,b]}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})\subseteq\textsf{Fun}^{\textsf{ex}}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})

be the full subcategory on exact functors of amplitude
[a,b]
,
and similarly for (half-)open intervals.
Let
wWCat
denote the category of weakly weighted categories and functors of bounded below amplitude.
Similarly, we let
\textsf{wWCat}_{=0}
resp.
\textsf{wWCat}_{\geq 0}
denote the category of weakly weighted
categories and exact functors that are weight exact resp. preserve connectives.
Note that these are not subcategories of
wWCat
(or each other)
since they do not share the same equivalences.

Definition 3.9
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a weakly weighted category.
We say that
\mathcal{C}
is left saturated if
\mathcal{C}_{\leq 0}={}^{\perp}{(\mathcal{C}_{\geq 1})}

and right saturated if
\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}={(\mathcal{C}_{\leq-1})}^{\perp}
.
We call
\mathcal{C}
saturated if it is both left and right saturated.

We now present three lemmas on the main advantages of saturation.
Namely, by virtue of being defines as orthogonal complements,
the (co)connectives in a saturated weakly weighted category
enjoy many more closure properties than required in the definition
(Lemma
3.10
).
Moreover, weight exact functors between saturated
weakly weighted categories behave more as one expects from
the theory of weighted categories (Lemma
3.12
).
Finally, we show that in the case
\ell<\infty
,
up to equivalence in
wWCat
(though not up to weight-exact equivalence!),
every weakly
\ell
-weighted category is saturated.

Lemma 3.10
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a weakly weighted category.
If
\mathcal{C}
is left
(
right
)
saturated, then
\mathcal{C}_{\leq 0}

(
\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}
)

is closed under the following operations, provided they exist:

(1)

retracts and extensions.

(2)

finite limits
(
finite colimits
)
.

(3)

arbitrary direct sums
(
arbitrary products
)
.

(4)

colimits
(
limits
)
of diagrams
X_{\bullet}\colon\gamma\to\mathcal{C}

(
X_{\bullet}\colon\gamma^{\textsf{op}}\to\mathcal{C}
)

for some ordinal
\gamma
satisfying

(a)

For a successor ordinal
\alpha+1<\gamma
,
the map
X_{\alpha}\to X_{\alpha+1}
has cofiber in
\mathcal{C}_{\leq 0}

(
the map
